def run_model(args) -> int:
    import operand_multihop3 as mh3
    import torch
    import torch.nn.functional as F
    import writeback_compile as wb
    from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer

    from verbum import jlens

    dev = (args.device if (args.device != "mps"
                           or torch.backends.mps.is_available()) else "cpu")
    rng = np.random.default_rng(args.seed)
    tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(args.model_id)
    if tok.pad_token is None:
        tok.pad_token = tok.eos_token
    tok.padding_side = "right"
    model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(
        args.model_id, dtype=getattr(torch, args.dtype)).to(dev).eval()
    for p in model.parameters():
        p.requires_grad_(False)
    dec, _norm, _lm_head = mh3.resolve_parts(model)
    nl = len(dec)
    tband = band_layers(nl)
    wband = list(range(round(tw.BAND_FRAC[0] * nl),
                       round(tw.BAND_FRAC[1] * nl) + 1))
    out_dir = Path(args.out)
    out_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    print(f"[ti] {args.model_id} dev={dev} n_layers={nl} "
          f"T-band=L{tband[0]}..L{tband[-1]} wire-band=L{wband[0]}..L{wband[-1]}")

    def surprisal(prefix: str, cont: str) -> float:
        pre = tok(prefix, return_tensors="pt").to(dev)
        full = tok(prefix + cont, return_tensors="pt").to(dev)
        n_pre = pre.input_ids.shape[1]
        with torch.no_grad():
            lo = model(**full).logits[0].float()
        lp = F.log_softmax(lo, dim=-1)
        tgt = full.input_ids[0]
        return -sum(float(lp[pos - 1, tgt[pos]])
                    for pos in range(n_pre, tgt.shape[0]))

    def capture_band(prefix: str) -> np.ndarray:
        """(L_band, d) residual at the last position of `prefix`."""
        resid, _ids = jlens.capture_residuals(model, tok, prefix)
        return np.stack([resid[li][-1].float().cpu().numpy() for li in tband])

    def arm_L(prefix_fn) -> tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:
        sA, sV = [], []
        for w, lb in zip(nonces, labels, strict=True):
            pre = prefix_fn(w, int(lb)) + f"The {w}"
            sA.append(np.mean([surprisal(pre, " " + p)
                               for p in tw.HELD_PREDS[0]]))
            sV.append(np.mean([surprisal(pre, " " + p)
                               for p in tw.HELD_PREDS[1]]))
        return np.array(sA), np.array(sV)

    def arm_T(prefix_fn) -> np.ndarray:
        return np.stack([capture_band(prefix_fn(w, int(lb)) + f"The {w}")
                         for w, lb in zip(nonces, labels, strict=True)])

    # ── nonce selection (type_write pattern) ──
    nonces, labels = [], []
    for i, w in enumerate(NONCE_CANDS):
        n_the = tok("The", add_special_tokens=False).input_ids
        n_thew = tok(f"The {w}", add_special_tokens=False).input_ids
        if len(n_thew) - len(n_the) >= 1:
            nonces.append(w)
            labels.append(i % 2)
    if args.n_nonce:
        a = [j for j, x in enumerate(labels) if x == 0][:args.n_nonce // 2]
        v = [j for j, x in enumerate(labels) if x == 1][:args.n_nonce // 2]

### Thinking

I'm working through the efficient residual capture approach: a single forward pass per arm extracts the full sequence residuals at once, which is much faster than multiple passes. Then I need to identify the probe positions by finding the token indices where each probe frame ends, which means tokenizing the input incrementally and tracking cumulative token counts.

The key challenge is handling tokenization boundaries correctly — since BPE merges tokens at string boundaries, concatenating pieces and tokenizing the whole sequence gives different results than tokenizing piecewise. The robust solution is to build the full text first, then use the tokenizer's offset mapping to convert character positions to token indices, which gives me the exact probe positions I need.

I also need to verify how capture_residuals handles special tokens and whether it matches my tokenization setup. Memory-wise, storing residuals from all 36 layers for ~1200 tokens is manageable if I immediately subselect just the T-band layers (L18–L30) and discard the rest. The runtime should be acceptable on the available hardware.

For the PRBS-6 sequence, I'm using an LFSR with taps x^6+x^5+1 to generate a length-63 pattern with ±1 values, and I need to confirm the cyclic autocorrelation is −1/63 off-peak. Each nonce gets a distinct cyclic shift—I'll use a deterministic scheme like shift = (3 + 5*i) % 63 to keep it reproducible. Then I'll iterate through coherent segments by cycling through the member statements for each word and class pairing.

For the control statements, I need templates that mention both the word and class without asserting membership—something like "A {w} is near the {cls}" keeps both elements present while using a spatial relationship instead of a predicate. cls coordination without explicit class membership. The filler slots match the main structure, so the control condition modulates how the class word and w interact. For the probe frame, I'm reading at the final w token with a neutral header to start the evidence segment. Then I'm computing a signed projection per nonce and probe position, taking the residual at that token, projecting onto the latent axes, comparing against the class band mean, and determining the sign based on class membership.

Now I'm removing the mean from each sequence, then computing a cross-correlation estimate using the standard PRBS formula with the ±1 modulation sequence. The scaling constant doesn't affect the gating comparison since it cancels out, so I'll stick with the (2/B) form to match the original specification. Finally, I'm summing the correlation values across the lag window to get the aggregate response.

For the cyclic correlation issue: the PRBS sequence wraps at the boundaries, which creates a small edge effect around 5% given the sequence length, but this is negligible and symmetric under null shifts, so I'll use the standard cyclic approach that PRBS theory assumes.

Now I'm setting up the null distribution by drawing 10,000 samples where each one randomly shifts the sequence by a non-trivial amount (between 4 and 59 positions to avoid the causal lag window) and computes the test statistic, then comparing the observed value against this distribution. For the paired test, I'm running a permutation test directly on the main and control statistics. For the scaling analysis, I'm fitting a power law to the mean statistics across three timescales to estimate the exponent.

When fitting the power law, I need to handle cases where the statistic becomes non-positive at shorter timescales—that's actually a signature of threshold behavior where the lock is lost. I'm bootstrapping across nonces to estimate the power law exponent and its confidence interval, and flagging cases where more than 25% of bootstrap samples show this threshold signature while the longest timescale remains positive.

The verdict logic splits into three categories: threshold behavior when the confidence interval excludes 1 from above, proportional when it contains 1, and compressive when the exponent is strictly less than 1. For the final classification, I'm marking it as threshold if that threshold-flavored signature appears, otherwise proportional—which also covers cases where the exponent is near 1 or unreadable from the frozen tree.

Now I'm moving to the real-member axis analysis for LK0, where I need to capture T-band residuals across 8 real members (4 animal and 4 vehicle) and validate them with a leave-one-out check. The approach is to build axes from the members themselves, then verify that the mean signed projection using leave-one-out scoring is positive, plus run a PRBS autocorrelation check to ensure the off-peak correlation stays below 0.1 and the values remain finite.

For the validation step, I'm setting up synthetic worlds with 20 nonces and B=63, where the tracker world uses shifted PRBS convolved with a short kernel to generate y traces with noise, while the control trace should produce near-zero response to test whether it properly fails the LK2 check.

I'm also defining several verdict worlds: PROPORTIONAL where the response scales with the signal, THRESHOLD where amplitude drops off at lower signal levels, LEXICAL-TRACK where both main and control traces match the filtered signal (passing LK1 but failing LK2), NOTRACK where everything is noise, and VOID where the member_loo value indicates a degenerate axis. The gates themselves are computed by a shared function that processes the y-traces, schedules, and member_loo values consistently across all worlds.

Now I'm setting up the output structure to save results in a dedicated directory with the model traces, schedules, and per-layer projections stored in compressed format. I'm also considering whether to store per-layer y values across the band dimension for depth profiling, which would be small but informative. For the CLI, I need to verify the model ID format and device/dtype arguments, mirroring whatever the type_icl setup uses.

I'm checking the argparse defaults across the different scripts to ensure consistency, particularly looking at how the model gets loaded in type_icl_tag and type_write, and confirming the import paths work correctly from the scripts directory. For the evidence segments, I'm joining them with spaces and appending the probe frame, making sure the spacing is handled correctly in the join logic. Now I'm ready to write the harness structure.

Setting up the core components: a pseudo-random binary sequence generator with period 63, schedule shifts, and segment builders for coherent, filler, and control segments. The main build_sequence function takes the word, class index, schedule, mode, and coherence fraction, then returns the text with probe character spans. For fractional coherence, I'm selecting exactly floor(s × n_plus) coherent slots from the positive schedule blocks using a deterministic random seed per nonce and arm.…

def capture_residuals(
    model: nn.Module,
    tokenizer: Any,
    text: str | None = None,
    *,
    input_ids: torch.Tensor | None = None,
) -> tuple[dict[int, torch.Tensor], torch.Tensor]:
    """Forward once; return ``({layer: (seq, d)}, input_ids)``.

    Provide ``text`` (tokenized here) or pre-tokenized ``input_ids`` (shape
    ``(seq,)`` or ``(1, seq)``) — the latter for synthetic streams (e.g.
    induction sequences of raw token ids). Residual = the *output* of each
    transformer layer (post-block), for every position. Reuses
    :func:`verbum.hooks.capture`.
    """
    if input_ids is not None:
        ids = input_ids if input_ids.dim() == 2 else input_ids.unsqueeze(0)
        inputs = {"input_ids": ids.to(model.device)}
    elif text is not None:
        inputs = tokenizer(text, return_tensors="pt").to(model.device)
    else:
        raise ValueError("capture_residuals needs `text` or `input_ids`")
    nl = n_layers(model)
    ivs = [hooks.capture(layer_path(model, i), name=str(i)) for i in range(nl)]
    with hooks.intervene(model, ivs) as sess:
        model(**inputs)
    out: dict[int, torch.Tensor] = {}
    for i in range(nl):
        out[i] = _hidden(sess.captured[str(i)])[0].float()  # (seq, d), on CPU
    return out, inputs["input_ids"][0].detach().cpu()

### Thinking

I'm setting up the tokenizer call with `return_offsets_mapping=True` to ensure the offset positions align with the input_ids, handling special tokens correctly regardless of the model's default behavior. Now I'll write out type_lockin.py with the essential components, keeping it concise but complete.

Defining the constants for the PRBS generator (degree 6 with taps [6,5], seed all-ones), lag windows, null/bootstrap sample counts, SNR levels, and the a priori dictionary and control templates. The shift generation uses a deterministic formula `shift_i = (5 + 11*i) % 63` where 11 is coprime to 63, guaranteeing distinct shifts across nonces. I'm also defining helper functions to pull coherent segments from the model's member statements, incoherent filler segments, and formatted control templates, then building the full sequence with probe character positions tracked. I need to map character positions to token indices using the tokenizer's offset mapping, ensuring that when I retokenize the same text with identical settings, the token IDs align properly with the captured residuals. Then I'm stacking the residual activations across layers at those probe positions to build the input for the projection.

For the demodulation step, I'm computing the cross-correlation between the centered output signal and the modulation schedule at different lags, summing across the relevant lag indices to get a demodulation strength metric. I'm doing this per arm and per nonce, then organizing everything into a bundle that includes the output signals, modulation schedules, leave-one-out membership indicators, and autocorrelation statistics before computing the gate values.

For the null hypothesis test, I'm generating 10,000 draws where each nonce's modulation schedule gets randomly shifted by an allowed amount, then recomputing the demodulation metric for each shifted version. The key optimization is precomputing the full cross-correlation function across all possible lags upfront, so that for any shift amount, I can just sum the appropriate lag indices rather than recomputing correlations from scratch each time.

Now I'm moving to the second test: a paired permutation test using the dsp.nulls function to compare the main and control demodulation metrics, checking whether the main signal is significantly greater.

For the third test, I'm computing the mean response across nonces for the main arm, then taking the cumulative sum of the step response to find the lock time—the first time constant where the cumsum reaches 0.63.

For the fourth test, I'm calculating the amplitude scaling across different arm settings (1.0, 0.5, 0.25) by bootstrapping over nonce indices, then fitting a power law to extract the scaling exponent from the log-log relationship.

Now I'm determining the category based on lock-loss fraction and the confidence interval of that exponent: if lock-loss is high, it's threshold-flavored; otherwise I check whether the exponent's CI suggests scaling behavior (above 1 means threshold-flavored, crossing 1 means proportional, below 1 means compressive). Finally, I'm applying the verdict logic where LK0 failure voids the result and missing LK1 means no tracking.

For validation, I'm synthesizing a test bundle with 20 draws and 63 bootstrap samples, rolling probability shifts and applying a convolution kernel to smooth the measurements before adding noise, then comparing the filtered signal against a control baseline. In the real scenario, the nominal schedule applies to both the SNR arms and the y_s measurement. For the threshold regime, the amplitude response shows a sharp transition where A(1)≈1, A(0.5)=0.1, and A(0.25)≈0, indicating lock loss when γ becomes large, whereas the proportional tracker maintains A proportional to the signal strength with γ≈1. Using bootstrap confidence intervals with n=20 and σ=0.3, I'm calculating the expected amplitude at A(0.25) as 0.25 times the filtered component, then comparing this against the noise-induced bootstrap standard deviation to assess the detection threshold.

Now I'm checking the noise correlation across the 63 samples with 4 lags—the standard deviation works out to roughly 0.15, and averaging over 20 runs gives about 0.034, so A(0.25)=0.5 is well above the noise floor and the γ confidence interval stays tight around 1. For the model run, I'm loading the model and tokenizer with the same nonce selection pattern across 20 runs, then capturing the real-member axis by extracting the context pattern and applying the appropriate prefix formatting to collect the hidden states.

For each nonce, I'm rolling the probe positions by the shift amount, then running through each arm (main, control, and the two scaled variants) to build the token sequences, extract residuals, and project them across all layers to get the per-layer predictions. The whole pipeline bundles these together, computes the gating verdict, and writes out the results to jsonl and npz files. Runtime is around 3 minutes for 80 captures, and while the residual storage across all 36 layers takes about 440 MB transiently on CPU, that's manageable.

The sequence length works out to roughly 1100-1300 tokens across the 63 blocks, and I'm making sure the token counts are roughly balanced between the member statements, incoherent text, and control conditions. For the smoke test I'm using 4 nonces and 2000 null samples just to verify the verdict prints correctly. One thing to keep in mind is how the probe frame "The {w}." gets inserted into the running prose — it joins with a space so the full sequence reads naturally, even though the probe sentence itself is a bit of a fragment.

I'm finalizing the control templates now, making sure they mirror the member statement structure with consistent patterns like "A {w} is near the {cls}." and "The {w} is beside a {cls}." — these cover the same grammatical variations. I also need to verify the idempotency module imports correctly from the same directory, since it pulls in type_write, holo_cap, and the nulls function from verbum.dsp.…

class Register(Enum):
    """λ measure verbatim: name the register before you build the probe."""
    routing = "routing"        # crisp/discrete: attention patterns, head selection
    value = "value"            # continuous/graded: residual content, subspaces
    contrast = "contrast"      # dark-field / difference channels (Q/M)
    magnitude = "magnitude"    # norms, energies, doses
    spectral = "spectral"      # eigen/singular structure
    causal = "causal"          # intervention -> outcome


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class NullDraws:
    """Draws from a declared null + provenance (recorded at construction)."""
    name: str
    draws: np.ndarray
    provenance: dict = field(default_factory=dict)

    def __post_init__(self):
        object.__setattr__(self, "draws", np.asarray(self.draws, dtype=float))
        if self.draws.size == 0:
            raise ValueError(f"null '{self.name}' produced zero draws")


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Gated:
    """The only object that carries a p-value. warnings NEVER alter data."""
    name: str
    value: float
    null_name: str
    null_mean: float
    null_std: float
    n_draws: int
    predict: str
    alpha: float
    p: float
    sign_ok: bool
    verdict: bool
    warnings: tuple[str, ...] = ()


def gate(value: float, null: NullDraws, predict: str, alpha: float = 0.05,
         name: str = "", claim_register: Register | None = None,
         probe_register: Register | None = None) -> Gated:
    """Compare an observed statistic against a declared null, directionally.

    predict: 'greater' (value predicted above null) or 'less'. Mandatory —
    there is no two-sided option (a prediction has a sign; λ yardstick).
    p is the add-one permutation p in the PREDICTED direction.
    verdict = (p < alpha) AND sign_ok. Wrong-sign extremity is a failure,
    reported verbatim, never flipped."""
    if not isinstance(null, NullDraws):
        raise TypeError("gate() requires a declared NullDraws (no null, no p)")
    if predict not in ("greater", "less"):
        raise ValueError("predict must be 'greater' or 'less' (declared a priori)")
    draws = null.draws
    v = float(value)
    if predict == "greater":
        p = float((1 + np.sum(draws >= v)) / (1 + draws.size))
        sign_ok = v > float(draws.mean())
    else:
        p = float((1 + np.sum(draws <= v)) / (1 + draws.size))
        sign_ok = v < float(draws.mean())
    warnings: list[str] = []
    if claim_register is not None and probe_register is not None \
            and claim_register is not probe_register:
        w = (f"register mismatch: claim={claim_register.value} "
             f"probe={probe_register.value} (s206 scar — verify the probe "
             f"measures the claimed quantity)")
        warnings.append(w)
        print(f"[dsp.gate] WARNING {name}: {w}", file=sys.stderr)
    return Gated(
        name=name, value=v, null_name=null.name,
        null_mean=float(draws.mean()), null_std=float(draws.std()),
        n_draws=int(draws.size), predict=predict, alpha=float(alpha),
        p=p, sign_ok=sign_ok, verdict=bool(p < alpha and sign_ok),
        warnings=tuple(warnings),
    )


# ── constructors ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
def shuffled_label(stat, y: np.ndarray, rng: np.random.Generator,
                   n_iter: int = 200) -> NullDraws:
    """Full shuffled-label pipeline null: stat(permuted labels), n_iter times.

    stat: callable(label_array) -> float. The stat must RERUN the whole
    downstream pipeline on the shuffled labels (the QK lesson: shuffle ->
    centroids -> subspace -> same mapping -> same statistic), not just
    re-score cached intermediates. NaN draws are dropped (recorded)."""
    draws = []
    for _ in range(n_iter):
        v = float(stat(rng.permutation(y)))
        if not np.isnan(v):
            draws.append(v)
    return NullDraws("shuffled_label", np.array(draws),
                     {"n_iter": n_iter, "n_kept": len(draws)})


def matched_random(stat, dim: int, norm: float, rng: np.random.Generator,
                   n_iter: int = 200) -> NullDraws:
    """Matched-norm random-direction null: stat(random unit vector * norm).

    The exact 3b/P-ATT-MED null family: same norm, isotropic direction."""
    draws = []
    for _ in range(n_iter):
        v = rng.standard_normal(dim)
        v *= norm / (np.linalg.norm(v) + 1e-12)
        draws.append(float(stat(v)))
    return NullDraws("matched_random", np.array(draws),
                     {"n_iter": n_iter, "dim": dim, "norm": float(norm)})


def paired_permutation(a: np.ndarray, b: np.ndarray, rng: np.random.Generator,
                       n_iter: int = 10000) -> NullDraws:
    """Paired sign-flip permutation null over mean(a - b) (paired by index).

    The P-TYPE-SWAP s288 arm-vs-arm statistic. Observed value = mean(a - b),
    computed by the caller; draws = mean under random per-pair sign flips."""
    diffs = np.asarray(a, dtype=float) - np.asarray(b, dtype=float)
    signs = rng.choice(np.array([-1.0, 1.0]), size=(n_iter, diffs.size))
    draws = (signs * diffs[None, :]).mean(axis=1)
    return NullDraws("paired_permutation", draws,
                     {"n_iter": n_iter, "n_pairs": int(diffs.size)})


def sign_flip(values: np.ndarray, rng: np.random.Generator,
              n_iter: int = 10000) -> NullDraws:
    """One-sample sign-flip null over mean(values) (H0: symmetric about 0).

    The 1c residual-sign discipline. Observed value = mean(values)."""
    v = np.asarray(values, dtype=float)
    signs = rng.choice(np.array([-1.0, 1.0]), size=(n_iter, v.size))
    draws = (signs * v[None, :]).mean(axis=1)
    return NullDraws("sign_flip", draws,
                     {"n_iter": n_iter, "n": int(v.size)})


def matched_range(stat, target: np.ndarray, rng: np.random.Generator,
                  n_iter: int = 200) -> NullDraws:
    """Matched-range null for geometric/spectral fits (λ yardstick MANDATORY
    gate for any approximate fit claim): stat(uniform draws over the target's
    observed range, same shape). If random values in the same range fit as
    well, describability != discovery (the s247 φ-ladder lesson)."""
    t = np.asarray(target, dtype=float)

gd in gates:\n        print(json.dumps(gd))\n    print(f\"VERDICT: {v}\")\n    return 0\n\n\ndef main() -> int:\n    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser(description=__doc__.splitlines()[0])\n ### Thinking

The LK1 test failed—the effect size came out negative when it should be positive, and the p-value was nowhere near significant. LK2 also showed no meaningful difference. But LK0 is working well with strong axis formation and ideal PRBS characteristics, so the core setup is sound. The run completed cleanly in 40 seconds.

The modulation component in section 1 is essentially dead without major rework—it had one shot to prove itself and missed. The synthesis metrics (sign and magnitude) remain independent and unaffected by this failure. Looking at the texture details, there's a small positive autocorrelation at lag 0 but negative values across lags 1–4, and the low-frequency dominance in the spectrum is just noise given that LK1 is null. The T register isn't tracking the evidence schedule above the matched shift-null baseline. The DC component wasn't tested here since it was mean-removed by design, but the behavioral accumulation pattern still holds across the slow ramp. This is a single-model readout at the block timescale, so results might differ if we used a different licensing-behavioral approach. Next step is to commit these results and prepare the closure batch for Michael's review.

Before finalizing, I'm running a sanity check on the instrument itself. LK1's null standard deviation is 0.42 across 20 nonces with an observed value of −0.157, which would be large if there were a real tracker with amplitude comparable to the member-axis scale, but the signal here is tiny relative to null noise. The test is properly scaled since the null standard deviation comes from the same traces, so the schedule-aligned correlation is indistinguishable from random alignment—this is decisive within the instrument's power. The p-value of 0.685 with the wrong sign gives no hint of a real effect, and the control is also null. The probe insulation appears to have worked since there's no lexical bleed at the AC level, meaning the register state at the probes stays flat. The remaining question is whether the evidence even moves the register at these probes at all, or if there's a detectable DC or step effect.

Now I'm checking whether the instrument might be measuring a dead channel—a false-negative risk if the evidence never moves the temperature at the probes even in aggregate. LK0 validated axis formation on real members but not the evidence-responsiveness of the nonce probes themselves. I can do a post-hoc check by comparing mean temperature at probes in the main arm (which has roughly 32 coherent statements) against the control arm, using the raw y projections stored before mean-removal. If the main arm shows a higher mean than the control, that would indicate the evidence does raise the temperature at the probes through a DC mechanism, even if the AC tracking is absent—suggesting an accumulate-and-hold behavior rather than continuous tracking.…

### Thinking

The modal arm test came back negative with no significant effect detected, though the baseline measurements look solid. I'll now document the DC contrast check to clarify whether the probes are actually responding to the signal or if there's an instrument issue.

### Thinking

Looking at the statistical results, the DC contrast effect is clearly significant — MAIN's mean own-class T substantially exceeds both the control and the lower dose conditions with strong p-values, and there's a clear dose-dependent relationship across the conditions. This confirms the channel isn't dead and that membership evidence does reliably increase own-class T responses in a dose-dependent manner.

However, there's no AC tracking happening — the register doesn't dynamically follow the schedule, which suggests the system operates as an integrator that accumulates evidence into a standing level rather than tracking modulation at the block timescale. The carrier lock picture doesn't fit the data, but the accumulate-and-hold model does, even though an ideal integrator would theoretically show AC correlation spread across positive lags, which the null shift test would only partially capture.

The real tension is that idempotency showed licensing rising within just 3 exposures — a response time well within the lag window — yet LK1 still fails to detect it with the wrong sign. This contradicts what I'd expect from a 3-block time constant integrator.…

### Thinking

I've confirmed that DC works while AC doesn't, with MAIN's standing T level significantly exceeding CTRL in a dose-dependent pattern, showing the channel is functional. Now I'll persist these results.

I'm also noting some important caveats to document, starting with a DC-alive advisory about evidence and probe dosing.

The key distinction here is that the failure isn't about evidence-responsiveness itself, but specifically about the tracking and AC claim—the register functions as an accumulate-and-hold device rather than a demodulator, which aligns with the idempotency patterns I'm seeing. There's a power limitation with LK1 against slow integrators, but the 3-block response window in the L register should theoretically cover what LK1 can detect, so if T tracked like L it should have shown up. I'm noting that a behavioral licensing-register AC read remains possible for v2, but I need to document this as a deferred prerequisite rather than letting the frame keep retreating. Finally, LK4 stays unread as intended since LK1 and LK2 conditions weren't met, and LK3's texture becomes irrelevant under the LK1 null result—what's dying with frames 0-3 is the carrier-lock and demodulation reading.

The frame is now dead per the pre-registered discipline: the must-win condition was spent on frames 0-3, so this frame is retired. What survives is the synthesis section on sign and magnitude, the retrodictions remain valid, and the negative result itself is actually a methodological win—the probe insulation held and the excitation measurement design worked as intended. I'm creating a memory note capturing the verdict that the type-register accumulates but doesn't track, along with the DC-alive advisory and the frame 0-3 status. I should add a new Results section to document the ledger and update the page status to reflect the frame retirement, though I'll skip updating the top-note blurb for now.

## Assistant

Sealed at `be8f9b45`. Session 326 closed two probes: one requalified a win, one killed a frame. Here's what we actually learned.

---

## What we learned today

### 1. The lattice concentrates by *subtraction*, not addition (growth-cancel split)

Yesterday's accumulation win said: early-committed weights end up ~2.7× larger than contested ones. The natural reading was "committed weights keep growing." Today we asked: **compared to what?** We built a baseline from the middle population — weights that committed mid-training — and decomposed the effect.

The answer inverted the emphasis: **~94% of the differential is churners collapsing, only ~6% is committed weights out-growing the baseline** — and that 6% is fragile (flips sign under a different baseline choice). Churners genuinely *shrink* in absolute terms (−0.42 log units) while everything committed grows.

So the picture is sculptural: **the lattice doesn't emerge because the signal is amplified — it emerges because everything contested cancels itself to nothing.** Training carves the figure by removing marble, not by adding clay. Combined with yesterday: the sign is the decision (made early, held), and magnitude differences arise mostly because *disagreement destroys*, not because agreement compounds.

### 2. The type register is a bucket, not a radio (lock-in)

The modulation frame's core claim was that a type judgment is a *demodulation event* — the model locks onto evidence like a receiver locks onto a carrier. That predicts something specific: if you flicker the evidence on and off in a known pattern, the register should flicker *with* it. That's the AC test, and no prior experiment had run it — everything before was DC ("is signal present at all?").

We ran it cleanly — PRBS-modulated evidence, readout at constant probe frames so no surface text could leak the schedule — and the answer is flatly **no**. The register shows zero response at the modulation schedule. Wrong sign, p = 0.685, with a fully sane instrument.

But the DC advisory rescued the *real* finding: the register **does** respond to evidence — its standing level rises dose-dependently with how much membership evidence it has seen (p = 0.0003, ordered exactly by dose). So:

> **The register integrates evidence into a standing level and holds it. It does not track. It's a bucket filling with water, not a radio following a station.**

### 3. The frame is dead — and that's the system working

The modulation frame is now **0–3** on pre-registered tests, with its named must-win spent. By your own s324 rule, it's done. What made it seductive was six retrodictions — it *explained* idempotency, disj-cost, gradedness beautifully. But every time it made a *forward* prediction, reality said no. That's the classic signature of an overfitted narrative: explains everything backward, predicts nothing forward. The measurements it "explained" all still stand — they just go back to being unexplained facts.

### The composite picture that survives (and it's coherent)

Interestingly, both results — from different substrates, different registers, different timescales — say the same thing:

- **Weights** (training): sign decided early, contested amplitude cancels to zero, evidence accumulates monotonically.
- **Tape** (inference): evidence accumulates into a standing level, doesn't oscillate back down.

Both faces are **integrators**. Nothing in this system demodulates; everything accumulates and holds. That's now the live thesis — "sign is the decision, magnitude is the evidence" — and it has a cheap testable edge already queued: *ternary survival should be predicted by commitment timing, not magnitude*. That's the sharpest next front, along with OLMo replication to check any of this generalizes past one model lineage.

One day, two closures, zero salvage narratives — and the surviving thesis is simpler than the one that died.

Ok so what we are seeing is more like a pile of photographs and not a negative film?


### Thinking

Michael's testing whether the data looks more like stacked photographs (accumulating exposures) or a photographic negative (where faint maps to common and dense to rare). The negative metaphor from §4 suggests weights should invert the function—high probability faint, high difficulty dense—but the thinking trails off before completing the comparison against what the actual data shows. The long-exposure photograph analogy crystallizes it perfectly — pedestrians and cars blur away through motion (contested votes cancel), while buildings remain sharp (consistent signal persists). That's exactly what the data shows: the lattice survives because it's stable across all measurements, everything else averages to noise. Michael's reframing captures this as co-addition rather than inversion — the image emerges from what all the exposures agree on, not from negating what's common. The metaphor works because it matches the measured mechanism: consistent coordinates accumulate linearly, contested ones cancel toward zero, leaving only the standing structure. The tape register accumulates like a camera sensor integrating photons—light only adds, never subtracts, so there's no oscillation or decay, just dose-ordered DC buildup. This kills the inversion element that stratigraphy ruled out: the commons aren't faint negatives but dense positives, and the stack preserves that density structure across frames.

For new predictions to avoid retrofitting: stacking should show SNR scaling with √N or N depending on coherence, and crucially, the order of exposures shouldn't matter—permuting which photographs stack first should yield the same standing level since integration is commutative. That's a clean falsifiable test: an integrator is order-invariant while a demodulator would be order-sensitive. So the key test is whether contradictory evidence actually cancels out symmetrically—a signed integrator would show net effects approaching zero when opposing signals balance, whereas a pure additive pile would just accumulate both sides. The weight mechanism suggests signed summation is at play, which means the system isn't like stacked photographs but more like a long-exposure photograph where moving objects create deviations that average toward the background. The distinction hinges on whether we're truly averaging or just adding.…

## Assistant

Yes — and the correction is precise enough to be worth stating carefully, because it names exactly which part of §4 died and which part survives.

**What died: the inversion.** A negative encodes by *reversal* — the more light (probability), the fainter the record. That was §4's distinctive claim: common = faint, the algorithm hides in the clear parts. Stratigraphy killed exactly that clause: the early-committed commons are *dense*, not faint. Whatever the plate is, it is not inverting anything.

**What the data actually looks like: a stacked long exposure.** Think astrophotography stacking, or the classic long-exposure photo of a busy street — the pedestrians and traffic vanish and you get an empty street with sharp buildings. Not because anything was inverted, but because:

- **What holds still across every frame accumulates.** Consistent structure reinforces with each exposure — that's the committed population, sign fixed early, amplitude stacking up (the thin-but-real growth clause).
- **What moves erases itself.** Deviations that point different directions on different frames average toward nothing — that's the churners netting to −0.42, the 94% of the effect. The empty street *is* the cancellation-dominated split.
- **The picture survives thresholding.** A hard high-contrast print of a stacked image still shows the scene, because the content lives in *where the consensus is*, not in how dark the print is — that's ternarizability without needing the dead faint-commons mechanism. Sign = "is there consensus structure at this pixel"; magnitude = how many frames agreed.

And today's lock-in result fits the same picture on the tape face: film during exposure only *integrates* — light adds, holds, never oscillates back out. The register behaved exactly like that: dose-ordered accumulation, zero tracking. A camera, not a radio, on both faces.

One sharpening, and one discipline note:
